      leptomeningeal melanoma.
    explanation: >-
      Four leptomeningeal melanomas among the six deaths in a fourteen-child series.

genetic:
- name: NRAS post-zygotic activating mutations
  gene_term:
    preferred_term: NRAS
    term:
      id: hgnc:7989
      label: NRAS
  relationship_type: CAUSATIVE
  variant_origin: SOMATIC
  notes: >-
    The causal lesion, found in affected skin and affected central nervous system tissue of the same
    patient and absent from their blood. Codon 61 is the hotspot; p.Q61K predominates.

    No ClinGen gene-disease validity assertion for this pair is cached here, so none is recorded. The
    relationship_type is the curator's reading of the primary literature: one series of fifteen patients
    with the mutation in twelve, in matched affected and unaffected tissue, plus a mouse model of the
    central nervous system arm.
  variants:
  - name: p.Q61K
    description: c.181C>A, the commoner of the two alleles, in ten of twelve mutation-positive patients.
  - name: p.Q61R
    description: c.182A>G, in two of twelve.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:23392294
    reference_title: >-
      Multiple congenital melanocytic nevi and neurocutaneous melanosis are caused by postzygotic
      mutations in codon 61 of NRAS.
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: >-
      These results suggest that single postzygotic NRAS mutations are responsible for multiple CMN
      and associated neurological lesions in the majority of cases.
    explanation: >-
      The authors' own statement of the gene-disease relationship, with their hedge: suggest, and in
      the majority of cases rather than all.
- name: BRAF
  gene_term:
    preferred_term: BRAF
    term:
      id: hgnc:1097
      label: BRAF
  relationship_type: CAUSATIVE
  variant_origin: SOMATIC
  notes: >-
    An alternative lesion in a minority. It matters clinically rather than only taxonomically: a
    BRAF-variant patient has a drug the NRAS ones do not. Graded CAUSATIVE on two cited sources
    rather than one - a prospective cohort in which 2 of the 16 patients with the neural lesion
    carried BRAF V600E and whose authors call it a novel finding, and a case report of sustained
    improvement on BRAF inhibition. The schema maps CAUSATIVE to ClinGen Definitive or Strong, and
    no external body has graded this pair, so read it as the curator's reading of two reports rather
    than as a copied tier; gene_disease_validity is deliberately empty for that reason.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:42384401
    reference_title: >-
      BRAF Inhibition in Congenital Nevi and Neural Melanosis.
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: INDIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: >-
      This case report describes sustained neurologic and cutaneous improvement following BRAF
      inhibition in a patient with BRAF V600E-variant neurocutaneous melanosis.
    explanation: >-
      A BRAF V600E patient, and the consequence of being one: sustained improvement on a BRAF
      inhibitor. One patient, which is why this row claims the alternative lesion exists and nothing
      about how often.
  - reference: PMID:25490715
    reference_title: >-
      BRAF mutations are also associated with neurocutaneous melanocytosis and large/giant
      congenital melanocytic nevi.
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: >-
      Neurocutaneous melanocytosis (NCM) affected 16 (24.2%) of 66 patients, with NRAS Q61 mutation
      in 12 (75.0%), and BRAF V600E in 2 (12.5%), P=0.009.
    explanation: >-
      How often, from a prospective cohort of 66 congenital melanocytic naevus patients: of the 16
      who had the neural lesion, 12 carried NRAS Q61 and 2 carried BRAF V600E. Two patients, which
      is the basis for recording BRAF as a causative alternative rather than only as a case report.
  - reference: PMID:25490715
    reference_title: >-
      BRAF mutations are also associated with neurocutaneous melanocytosis and large/giant
      congenital melanocytic nevi.
    supports: SUPPORT

    explanation: >-

imaging_findings:
- name: T1 hyperintensity of parenchymal melanosis
  modality: MRI
  imaging_finding_term:
    preferred_term: T1 hyperintensity before contrast
    term:
      id: NCIT:C182328
      label: Hyperintensity
  description: >-
    Bright signal on T1-weighted images, before any contrast is given, where melanin is likely
    deposited in the central nervous system. In the Japanese screening series of infants the lesions
    were seen most often in the amygdala, then the cerebellum, the brainstem and the cerebral
    hemispheres; the series' abstract gives no counts by site. A case report's background names the
    amygdala, pons and cerebellum as the commonest sites, says melanosis can also be seen elsewhere
    in the central nervous system, including the spinal cord and cauda equina, and calls the signal
    typically bilateral.
  located_in:
    preferred_term: central nervous system
    term:
      id: UBERON:0001017
      label: central nervous system
  notes: >-
    Timing matters. In two neonates, aged 11 and 23 days, the melanosis was not hidden by
    myelination and showed without contrast; over the middle and later months of the first year it
    becomes harder to see, and contrast-enhanced T1-weighted sequences may be needed. A scan without
    these changes does not exclude the disease: the case report's background cites patients with
    normal MRI and melanin deposits found at histopathology, and a review describes patients with or
    without the typical changes. T2 signal is reported both ways: the case report found its lesions
    bright on T2 and FLAIR and sets this against reports of decreased T2 and FLAIR signal from
    melanin, so only the T1 appearance is recorded as characteristic.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:37777590
    reference_title: >-

      melanocytic nevi.

    directness: DIRECT

- name: Leptomeningeal thickening with T1 hyperintensity
  modality: MRI
  imaging_finding_term:
    preferred_term: leptomeningeal thickening with T1 hyperintensity
    term:
      id: HP:0010651
      label: Abnormal meningeal morphology
  description: >-
    MRI may show melanosis of the leptomeninges as thickening around the brain and spinal cord with
    high signal on T1-weighted images. A 2024 imaging article separates this diffuse leptomeningeal
    form from focal melanosis of the brain parenchyma, the more prevalent, because the two carry
    different risks: focal melanosis is more likely to cause epileptic seizures, and diffuse
    melanosis is significantly more often associated with hydrocephalus or even cerebral melanoma.
  located_in:
    preferred_term: leptomeninges
    term:
      id: UBERON:0000391
      label: leptomeninx
  spatial_extent: DIFFUSE
  notes: >-
    Bound to HP:0010651 Abnormal meningeal morphology, the narrowest HPO term that covers thickening
    of the leptomeninges around both the brain and the spinal cord. Its nearest child terms, as OLS
    listed them on 20 November 2026, name contrast enhancement (HP:0032070 Leptomeningeal
    enhancement), one layer (HP:0100700 Abnormal arachnoid mater morphology, HP:0100701 Abnormal pia
    mater) or the spinal meninges only (HP:0010303 Abnormal spinal meningeal morphology). OLS
    searches of HPO and NCIT on 20 November 2026 for "leptomeningeal thickening", "meningeal
    thickening" and "thickened meninges" returned no term for leptomeningeal thickening. NCIT's two
    leptomeningeal disease findings (NCIT:C162991 Classical Leptomeningeal Disease Finding,
    NCIT:C162992 Nodular Leptomeningeal Disease Finding) are defined as diffuse spread of neoplastic
    cells to the leptomeninges, which may show as linear or nodular enhancement on MRI, so they do
    not fit either.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:41474606
    reference_title: >-
      Prenatal Fetal Neurocutaneous Melanosis: A Case Report and Literature Review.
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: REVIEW_SYNTHESIS
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: >-
      MRI might reveal meningeal thickening surrounding the brain and spinal cord, with
      characteristic high signal intensity on T1-weighted sequences and low signal intensity on
      T2-weighted sequences, indicating the presence of melanin and suggesting a diagnosis of NCM.
    explanation: >-
      The appearance of the leptomeningeal form, from the literature review of a fetal case report.
  - reference: PMID:39518313
    reference_title: >-
      Central Nervous System Involvement and Neuroradiological Imaging Insights of Neurocutaneous
      Melanocytosis in Congenital Melanocytic Nevi.
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: REVIEW_SYNTHESIS
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: >-
      When the CNS is involved, it is crucial to differentiate between focal melanosis of the brain
      parenchyma, which is more prevalent, and disseminated or diffuse melanosis of the
      leptomeninges. While focal melanosis is more likely to precipitate epileptic seizures, diffuse
      melanosis is significantly more frequently associated with complications such as hydrocephalus
      or even cerebral melanoma formation
    explanation: >-
      The distinction between the two forms and the different risks the article attaches to each.
diagnosis:
- name: Screening brain and spine magnetic resonance imaging
  diagnosis_term:
    preferred_term: magnetic resonance imaging of the neuraxis
    term:
      id: NCIT:C16809
      label: Magnetic Resonance Imaging
  description: >-
    The test that finds the neural half, and the one argued about. Two screening series find an
    abnormality in a fifth to a third of the children they scanned, depending on the series and on
    what counts as an abnormality: the lesion on brain MRI in 31.6% of Japanese infants starting
    treatment for congenital melanocytic naevi, and an abnormal scan of any kind in 21% of the
    children born with more than one naevus whom one centre scanned. The abstracts of those series
    do not say what proportion were symptom-free. Whether finding the lesion changes anything is the
    open question this entry records. The cited recommendations differ on whom to scan. One centre's
    criterion since 2008 is a single MRI for every child born with more than one congenital naevus,
    whatever the size or site of the largest; the Nevosurgery Network recommends MRI for a giant
    naevus over 40 cm projected adult size, more than 20 concomitant naevi, or neurological symptoms
    needing clarification. A normal scan does not remove the risk of melanoma: in an earlier report
    from the same group, on children seen from 1991 to 2007, two children whose scans were normal
    died of it.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:37777590
    reference_title: >-
      Incidence of neurocutaneous melanosis in Japanese pediatric patients with congenital
      melanocytic nevi.
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: >-
      Japanese pediatric patients with CMN under 1 year of age were included between January 2020
      and November 2022, and all patients underwent brain MRI to check for NCM in this study.
    explanation: >-
      The screening design: every infant imaged, which is what makes the 31.6% figure a prevalence
      in that group rather than a yield in the symptomatic.
  - reference: PMID:25966033
    reference_title: >-
      Classification of neurological abnormalities in children with congenital melanocytic naevus
      syndrome identifies magnetic resonance imaging as the best predictor of clinical outcome.
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: >-
      For screening for congenital neurological lesions a single MRI in multiple CMN is a clinically
      relevant strategy.
    explanation: >-
      The conclusion of the screened cohort (289 children met its criterion for a scan), which is as
      close to an answer as the cited literature comes. It says the scan is a relevant strategy, not
      that acting on it changes the course; the entry's discussion records that second question as
      the one still open.
  - reference: PMID:25966033
    reference_title: >-
      Classification of neurological abnormalities in children with congenital melanocytic naevus
      syndrome identifies magnetic resonance imaging as the best predictor of clinical outcome.
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: >-
      Of 376 patients seen between 1991 and 2013, 289 fulfilled our criterion for a single screening
      CNS MRI, which since 2008 has been more than one CMN at birth, independent of size and site of
      the largest naevus.
    explanation: >-
      The first policy: the centre's screening criterion, more than one congenital naevus at birth
      whatever the size or site of the largest.
  - reference: PMID:39518313
    reference_title: >-
      Central Nervous System Involvement and Neuroradiological Imaging Insights of Neurocutaneous
      Melanocytosis in Congenital Melanocytic Nevi.
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: REVIEW_SYNTHESIS
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: >-
      The Nevosurgery Network, therefore, recommends MRI examinations in cases of GCMN (>40 cm
      projected adult size (PAS)), and/or the presence of >20 concomitant nevi, and/or neurological
      symptoms requiring clarification

- name: Mutation testing of lesional tissue
  description: >-
    Blood will not carry the mutation, so the test has to be on lesional tissue - naevus,
    leptomeninges, or cultured cells from either. This is the practical consequence of mosaicism and
    the reason a negative blood test means nothing here. One cited source, on primary diffuse
    leptomeningeal melanomatosis, reports that the tumour's somatic mutation could be detected in
    cerebrospinal fluid and offers this as a potential biomarker, a route that would need no
    lesional biopsy.
  notes: >-
    No diagnosis_term is bound. The sibling rows carry NCIT clinical-action terms, and the nearest one
    available here names immunohistochemistry, which is the wrong kind of test for this row; binding it
    would assert a method this row is not about. The preferred term for the action is carried by the
    row name instead.
  evidence:

    explanation: >-
      The test performed on lesional material rather than on blood, here to confirm that cultured
      cells came from the lesion. INDIRECT for the diagnostic claim: the paper's purpose is a cell
      model, not a diagnostic workflow.

    explanation: >-

differential_diagnoses:
- name: Melanoma metastatic to the leptomeninges
  description: >-
    Melanoma that has spread to the leptomeninges from a primary cutaneous tumour. A series of
    primary leptomeningeal melanocytic neoplasms names it as the differential diagnosis its genetic
    findings may help with, for primary tumours like those it studied. The 1991 diagnostic criteria
    of this disease exclude a patient whose skin and meningeal lesions are both malignant: they
    allow a cutaneous melanoma only where the meningeal lesions are histologically benign, and a
    meningeal melanoma only where the cutaneous lesions are benign.
  disease_term:
    preferred_term: melanoma metastatic to the leptomeninges
    term:

- name: Meningeal melanocytoma
  description: >-
    A primary melanocytic tumour of the meninges. In a series of 20 primary leptomeningeal
    melanocytic neoplasms, from melanocytomas to melanomas, most carried a GNAQ or GNA11 mutation
    and none an NRAS, BRAF or HRAS hotspot mutation. A later series from the same group sets this
    disease against them: in it, large or multiple congenital naevi accompany the leptomeningeal
    tumour, and the same NRAS mutation is frequently found in both.
  disease_term:
    preferred_term: meningeal melanocytoma

      id: MONDO:0016746
      label: meningeal melanocytoma
  distinguishing_features:
  - no congenital melanocytic naevi
  - GNAQ or GNA11 mutation in most of a mixed series, and no NRAS, BRAF or HRAS hotspot mutation

- name: Primary leptomeningeal melanoma without congenital naevi
  description: >-
    Primary melanoma of the leptomeninges in a patient without congenital naevi. The 2015 series of
    primary leptomeningeal melanocytic neoplasms, which includes melanomas, found a GNAQ or GNA11
    mutation in most and no NRAS hotspot mutation; the later series from the same group, whose
    patients were all adults, sets this disease against them for its frequent NRAS mutation. The
    exception to watch is a child: in the trametinib series, whose four children all had
    NRAS-mutated melanoma, a child without skin lesions had a leptomeningeal melanoma
    indistinguishable from those of the children with naevi, which its authors suggest could be the
    same post-zygotic mutation affecting only the nervous system.

treatments:
- name: Cerebrospinal fluid shunting
  therapeutic_modality: SURGERY
  treatment_term:
    preferred_term: cerebrospinal fluid diversion
    term:
      id: NCIT:C93208
      label: Cerebrospinal Fluid Diversion
  description: >-
    The commonest operation in this disease, done for the hydrocephalus rather than for the melanocytic
    lesion. It relieves the pressure and leaves everything upstream of it untouched.
  target_mechanisms:
  - target: Obstruction of Cerebrospinal Fluid Flow
    description: >-
      Diverting cerebrospinal fluid bypasses the obstructed pathway. It acts on no step above that node:
      the mutant clone, the melanocytosis and the risk of transformation are all unaddressed, which is
      why a shunted child is not a treated child.
    evidence:
    - reference: PMID:29948137
      reference_title: >-
        Neurocutaneous melanosis presenting with hydrocephalus and malignant transformation:
        case-based update.
      supports: SUPPORT
      directness: INDIRECT
      quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
      evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
      snippet: >-
        Patient required placement of right-sided ventriculoperitoneal shunt to control
        hydrocephalus. The patient tolerated the procedure well and was discharged home with
        normal neurological function.
      explanation: >-
        The operation acting on this node in one infant, with the outcome that makes the point:
        normal neurological function on discharge, and the same child died of malignant
        transformation of the leptomeningeal disease the shunt did not touch.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:35535823
    reference_title: >-
      Neurosurgical management of patients with neurocutaneous melanosis: a systematic review.
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: >-
      The most common surgical technique was CSF shunting (43%, 24/56 operations), with tethered
      cord release (4%, 2/56 operations) being the least frequently performed.
    explanation: >-
      How often it is done, across 74 pooled published patients.
  notes: >-
    No cited source compares shunting against not shunting. The row records practice and the mechanism
    it acts on.
- name: MEK inhibition
  therapeutic_modality: SMALL_MOLECULE
  treatment_term:
    preferred_term: Pharmacotherapy
    term:
      id: NCIT:C15986
      label: Pharmacotherapy
    therapeutic_agent:
    - preferred_term: trametinib
      term:
        id: CHEBI:75998
        label: trametinib
    - preferred_term: binimetinib
      term:
        id: CHEBI:145371
        label: binimetinib
  description: >-
    Blocking the pathway the mutation activates. The clinical evidence is case-level: single case
    reports, one centre's series of four children with NRAS-mutated melanoma of the central nervous
    system, and a registry study of melanoma of the central nervous system in this disease, none
    with a controlled comparison. In the series, where three of the four children had congenital
    naevi, every child improved in symptoms and signs on trametinib for between one and nine months,
    with no clinically significant side effects and, in one child, stable leptomeningeal disease on
    MRI at 56 and 139 days; every child then progressed through treatment and died. In the registry
    study, with published cases added, none of 27 children survived a year, including the nine given
    trametinib among other treatments, and 14 of the 16 with data had an NRAS alteration. The single
    patients did not go the same way. One infant's leptomeningeal thickening fell rapidly on
    trametinib with azacitidine and he was developmentally normal at 53 months. Another child took
    trametinib for seven months and eventually died of malignant transformation of his
    leptomeningeal disease. A 13-year-old died five days after binimetinib was started, too soon for
    a clinical effect, though his tissue showed lower pERK and Ki67 afterwards; the 2014 report
    calls the drug MEK162, which is binimetinib. A 13-year-old girl with melanosis of the amygdala
    and medial temporal lobes, seen only on imaging, had no further neurological decline at one-year
    follow-up, four to five months after trametinib was added to levetiracetam; within about two
    months of starting trametinib she had a breakthrough seizure-like episode and her levetiracetam
    dose was raised. Her skin changed in both directions: by her ten-month follow-up, when she had
    completed 10 weeks of trametinib, the naevus on her forehead had shrunk from 3.1 to 2.7 cm and
    the borders of her largest naevus looked lighter, while some of her other naevi were reportedly
    darker. A child with primary diffuse leptomeningeal melanomatosis, an aggressive cancer
    biologically closely related to this disease, received trametinib and everolimus, followed by
    nivolumab and ipilimumab with intraventricular chemotherapy; the child improved temporarily and
    survived seven months, and no part of that result can be attributed to the MEK inhibitor alone.
  target_mechanisms:
  - target: Post-zygotic NRAS Codon 61 Mutation
    description: >-
      The drug cannot undo the mutation; it acts on the pathway the mutation holds open, which this node
      binds as increased Ras protein signal transduction. Recorded against this node rather than a
      downstream one because that is where the target sits.
    evidence:
    - reference: PMID:24713450
      reference_title: >-
        Experimental treatment of NRAS-mutated neurocutaneous melanocytosis with MEK162, a
        MEK-inhibitor.
      supports: SUPPORT
      directness: INDIRECT
      quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
      evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
      snippet: >-
        We observed lower MIB-1 expression and lower pERK expression in the post-treatment samples
        compared to pre-treatment, suggesting a potential effect of MEK inhibiting therapy.
      explanation: >-
        The pathway readout in one patient's own tumour before and after the drug, with the
        authors' hedge: suggesting a potential effect. The same patient died five days into
        treatment, so this is a molecular observation and not a clinical result.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:28253523
    reference_title: >-
      MEK inhibition appears to improve symptom control in primary NRAS-driven CNS melanoma in
      children.
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: INDIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: >-
      All four had an improvement in symptoms and objectively in signs. These varied from mild
      improvement for 1 month, to a sustained symptom-free period of 9 months in one case.
    explanation: >-
      The series' result in four children with NRAS-mutated melanoma of the central nervous system,
      three of them with congenital naevi by the series' own table: a temporary improvement in every
      child. Indirect for this disease as a whole, because the children had melanoma of the central
      nervous system and one had no naevi.
  - reference: PMID:28253523
    reference_title: >-
      MEK inhibition appears to improve symptom control in primary NRAS-driven CNS melanoma in

    supports: REFUTE
    directness: INDIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: >-
      In all cases there was eventual disease progression through treatment, followed by rapid death

    explanation: >-
      The same four children later: the improvement did not last, and every child died. REFUTE

  - reference: PMID:28253523

    supports: SUPPORT

    reference_title: >-

      at melanoma diagnosis (range: 0.2-16.6). Fourteen of 16 cases with reported data had a NRAS

      longer than 1 year after diagnosis despite multimodal therapy (including trametinib; n = 9),

    explanation: >-

  - reference: PMID:29948137

  - reference: PMID:33921303
    reference_title: >-
      Novel Insights into Diagnosis, Biology and Treatment of Primary Diffuse Leptomeningeal
      Melanomatosis.
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: INDIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: >-
      We report the first case of a pediatric patient with PDLMM who received combination treatment
      including trametinib and everolimus, followed by intravenous nivolumab and ipilimumab with
      concomitant intensive intraventricular chemotherapy, resulting in temporary significant
      clinical improvement and overall survival of 7 months.
    explanation: >-
      A child with primary diffuse leptomeningeal melanomatosis, an aggressive cancer biologically
      closely related to this disease. The MEK inhibitor was given with everolimus and followed by
      two immunotherapies with intraventricular chemotherapy, so neither the temporary improvement
      nor the seven-month survival can be attributed to it. INDIRECT for that reason.
  notes: >-
    None of the clinical reports cited here makes a controlled comparison. The patients described
    here come from single case reports, one centre's series of four and a national registry study
    whose expanded cohort adds published cases, so some children may be counted more than once. In
    the series the benefit was temporary in every child, and every child in the series and in the
    registry study's cohort died. Of the patients cited here, only the infant given azacitidine and
    trametinib together is reported well years later. A phase II trial of mirdametinib for large and
    giant congenital naevi is registered (NCT07891286) and not yet recruiting; its primary endpoint
    is the size of the skin naevus, and it admits people with neurocutaneous melanocytosis only if
    that is asymptomatic and not progressing. The comparison between inhibitors is in cells and is
    recorded on the next row.
- name: PI3K and mTOR pathway inhibition
  therapeutic_modality: SMALL_MOLECULE
  treatment_term:
    preferred_term: Pharmacotherapy
    term:
      id: NCIT:C15986
      label: Pharmacotherapy

      term:

        label: omipalisib

      term:
        id: CHEBI:231324
        label: alpelisib

      term:
        id: CHEBI:68478
        label: everolimus
  description: >-
    The other arm of the pathway the mutation opens. In nevospheres grown from four patients' own
    spinal cord, brain and skin lesions, the dual PI3K/mTOR inhibitor GSK2126458 reduced viability
    further than any other inhibitor tested - below half. One cited patient received an mTOR
    inhibitor, everolimus, together with trametinib and followed by two immunotherapies with
    intraventricular chemotherapy, so nothing can be attributed to it. One centre reports that
    another mTOR inhibitor, sirolimus, had no measurable effect in three children with melanoma of
    the central nervous system.

    matters: alpelisib was tested on naevus explants from ten patients and not on the neural lesion,
    and everolimus appears in the cited literature only with trametinib, followed by two
    immunotherapies, in one melanomatosis patient.

clinical_trials:
- name: NCT07891286
  phase: PHASE_II
  status: NOT_RECRUITING
  description: >-
    A single-group phase II study of the MEK inhibitor mirdametinib, taken by mouth in four-week
    cycles for up to two years, in people aged 3 to 40 with large or giant congenital melanocytic
    naevi that carry a somatic NRAS or BRAF variant or one of the gene fusions the record lists. Its
    primary endpoint is an objective decrease in the largest diameter of the naevus. The registry
    lists neurocutaneous melanocytosis among its conditions and admits people with it only if it is
    asymptomatic and not progressing, and participants have MRI of the brain and spinal cord.
    Registered by the US National Cancer Institute as not yet recruiting, with an estimated start in
    January 2027 and an estimated enrolment of 15; NOT_RECRUITING is the closest status the schema
    has.
  target_phenotypes:
  - preferred_term: large or giant congenital melanocytic naevi
    term:
      id: HP:0000995
      label: Melanocytic nevus
  notes: >-
    Registry fields as retrieved from ClinicalTrials.gov on 20 November 2026. The primary endpoint
    is the skin naevus. The secondary outcomes include pERK as a pharmacodynamic marker in serial
    biopsies of the naevus and whether melanoma occurs, and none is named for the leptomeningeal or
    parenchymal lesion.

progression:
- phase: Congenital cutaneous lesion
  notes: >-
    The naevi are present at birth and are what prompts everything else. Nothing neurological is
    expected at this point. How many go on to develop symptoms is not settled by the cited sources
    and the entry does not assert a majority either way: in the registry series, 17 of the 26
    patients reported to have the neural lesion also reported neurological symptoms, while the
    abstract of the imaging series that found the lesion in a third of infants gives no symptom
    data.

- phase: Asymptomatic central nervous system involvement
  notes: >-
    Found only by imaging. One series that scanned every infant, 38 Japanese infants under one year
    treated for congenital melanocytic naevi, found the lesion on brain MRI in about a third, and in
    a quarter of those not previously examined or treated; its abstract does not say how many had
    symptoms. Whether this phase progresses is the question the entry's screening discussion
    records; the 14-patient referral series includes a child with focal seizures and a normal scan,
    so the two do not map onto each other.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:37777590

      not say how many had symptoms, so for this phase the figures are an upper bound for lesions
      seen on brain MRI.
- phase: Symptomatic neurological disease
  notes: >-
    Acute in infancy or, more often, later in childhood or adult life - though the 1991 review that set
    the criteria found most of its 39 cases presenting in the first two years. Once symptoms appear
    the outlook changes sharply: a systematic review states in its framing that patients typically
    die within three years of symptom onset, and the 1991 review found the prognosis extremely poor
    even without melanoma. Read that against the phase above:
    the figure is conditioned on becoming symptomatic, and what proportion of those with the lesion
    become symptomatic is not established by any cited source.

prevalence:

  measure_type: POINT_PREVALENCE
  prevalence_class: ABOVE_1_IN_1000
  rate_per_100000: 31600.0
  rate_denominator: POPULATION
  notes: >-
    Not a population prevalence, and the denominator is what makes it meaningful: 38 infants
    starting treatment for congenital melanocytic naevi, all of whom were imaged. The quantity is
    the proportion of that group with the lesion on brain MRI, symptomatic or not, which is why the
    class reads as common and would be absurd read as a figure for the general population. The other
    proportions in this section measure other quantities in other groups, so they are recorded side
    by side rather than averaged.

    involvement only - 4.8% of the 186 registry patients who had magnetic resonance imaging - where

    does not close the gap: the same registry puts involvement of any kind at 26 of 379. This study
    is also self-reported, which its own limitations section states, while the row above is
    prospective imaging of every infant enrolled.

animal_models:
- name: Embryonic melanocyte NRAS(G12D) mouse
  species: Mouse
  genotype: oncogenic NRAS (G12D) expressed in melanocytes of developing embryos
  publication: PMID:23303902
  description: >-
    Oncogenic NRAS driven in melanocytes during embryogenesis. It produces the congenital melanocytic
    lesions and the central nervous system melanoma of this disease, and it does not produce cutaneous
    melanoma.
  notes: >-
    No MGI identifier is bound for the targeted gene. The construct is human-numbered oncogenic NRAS

    the genotype string rather than bound to a model-organism identifier that would have to be written
    from memory.
  modeled_mechanisms:
  - target: Mosaic Melanocytic Clone Spanning Skin and Neuraxis
    relationship: PARTIALLY_RECAPITULATES
    fidelity: MODERATE
    model_scale: CELLULAR
    description: >-
      Reproduces the embryonic timing and the two-compartment distribution: congenital melanocytic
      lesions in the skin and melanocytic disease in the central nervous system from one driver.
    limitations: >-
      The allele is G12D. Every mutation-positive patient in the series that established the lesion
      carries a codon 61 substitution instead - twelve of fifteen patients, the other three being
      mutation-negative - and a further cited cohort finds BRAF V600E rather than NRAS in two of
      sixteen, and the
      mouse's cutaneous lesions are described as resembling blue naevi rather than congenital melanocytic
      naevi. The mouse is a conditional transgenic rather than a mosaic, so the one feature that defines
      the human disease - mutant and wild-type cells side by side from a post-zygotic event - is not
      what the model has.
    divergences:
    - divergence_type: PROXY_QUANTITY
      materiality: QUALIFYING
      description: >-
        The model's quantity is NRAS G12D expressed from a transgene in a melanocyte lineage. The node's
        quantity is a codon 61 substitution present post-zygotically in one clone among wild-type cells.
    - divergence_type: SPECIES_MISMATCH
      materiality: QUALIFYING
      description: >-
        The cutaneous lesion the mouse makes is reported as resembling human blue naevus, which is not
        the congenital melanocytic naevus this node's descendants form in patients.

      explanation: >-

discussions:
- discussion_id: ncm_screening_mri_value
  kind: KNOWLEDGE_GAP
  status: OPEN
  prompt: >-
    Does screening every infant with more than one congenital melanocytic naevus for central nervous
    system involvement change what happens to them?
  attaches_to:
  - diagnosis#Screening brain and spine magnetic resonance imaging
  - progression#Asymptomatic central nervous system involvement
  rationale: >-
    Imaging finds an abnormality in a fifth to a third of the children scanned in the two screening
    series the diagnosis row names, depending on the series and on what counts as an abnormality. No
    cited source shows that finding it early changes the course, and no treatment has been shown to
    change the course. What the cited sources do establish is weaker and still useful: an abnormal
    scan is the most significant predictor of abnormal neurodevelopment, seizures and the need for
    neurosurgery in the largest screened cohort, and that cohort's authors conclude a single
    screening scan is a clinically relevant strategy. Prediction is not benefit, which is why this
    stays a gap rather than a recommendation. The study that set out to answer this names the
    question in its own objective. Against that, the entry's own progression rows record that
    patients with the symptomatic form typically die within three years of symptom onset, so the
    argument for finding it early is not nothing - it is untested. The entry's four prevalence
    records among people with congenital melanocytic naevi span 4.8% to 31.6%, partly because they
    do not all count the same thing - one is asymptomatic involvement only - so the size of the
    screened-positive group is not settled either.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:25966033
    reference_title: >-
      Classification of neurological abnormalities in children with congenital melanocytic naevus
      syndrome identifies magnetic resonance imaging as the best predictor of clinical outcome.
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: >-
      Abnormal MRI was the most significant predictor of all outcome measures.
    explanation: >-
      What the screened cohort established, which is prediction rather than benefit: the scan
      predicts abnormal neurodevelopment, seizures and the need for neurosurgery. The gap this
      discussion records is the step from predicting to changing.
  - reference: PMID:16310055
    reference_title: >-
      Asymptomatic neurocutaneous melanocytosis in patients with large congenital melanocytic nevi:
      a study of cases from an Internet-based registry.
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: BACKGROUND
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: >-
      Recent retrospective studies using magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) to screen for
      neurocutaneous melanocytosis (NCM) among neurologically asymptomatic children with large
      congenital melanocytic nevi (LCMN) report high prevalence (23-30%) of asymptomatic NCM. We
      sought to determine prevalence of asymptomatic NCM, and current application of MRI as a
      screening tool.
    explanation: >-
      The size of the asymptomatic group as the literature had it, and a second study setting out to
      measure it - which then reported 4.8%. The gap between those figures is part of why the
      question stays open.
- discussion_id: ncm_targeted_therapy_untested
  kind: KNOWLEDGE_GAP
  status: OPEN
  prompt: >-
    Does inhibiting the pathway the NRAS mutation activates change the course of the neural disease?
  attaches_to:
  - treatments#MEK inhibition
  - treatments#PI3K and mTOR pathway inhibition
  rationale: >-
    The pathway is known, the drugs exist, and the evidence stops at case reports, one centre's
    series and a registry study, none with a controlled comparison. The series' four children, three
    of them with congenital naevi, had melanoma of the central nervous system; each improved on
    trametinib for one to nine months and then progressed through treatment and died, and in the
    registry study's cohort of 27 children with melanoma of the central nervous system in this
    disease, which adds published cases, none survived a year, including the nine given trametinib
    among other treatments. Of the single patients, one infant's leptomeningeal thickening fell
    rapidly on trametinib and he was developmentally normal at 53 months, but he received
    azacitidine at the same time. Another child took trametinib for seven months and died of
    malignant transformation of his leptomeningeal disease. A 13-year-old received a MEK inhibitor
    and died five days later, too soon for an effect, though his tissue showed reduced pERK
    afterwards. A teenager with parenchymal melanosis seen on imaging had no further decline at
    one-year follow-up, four to five months after trametinib was added to levetiracetam. A child
    with primary diffuse leptomeningeal melanomatosis, an aggressive cancer biologically closely
    related to this disease, received trametinib and everolimus followed by two immunotherapies with
    intraventricular chemotherapy, improved temporarily and survived seven months, so nothing in
    that course can be attributed to the MEK inhibitor either. MEK inhibition has relieved the
    symptoms of melanoma for a time; whether it changes the course of the benign neural lesion has
    not been tested in any comparison the cited reports describe. The cell-culture comparisons point
    at the PI3K/mTOR arm rather than the MEK arm, and the compound that performed best there has
    never been given to a patient in the cited literature. A phase II trial of mirdametinib for
    large and giant naevi is registered (NCT07891286) but not yet recruiting; its primary endpoint
    is the skin naevus, and it admits people with neurocutaneous melanocytosis only if that is
    asymptomatic; the disease is rare enough that a trial of the neural disease would have to be
    international.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:24713450
    reference_title: >-
      Experimental treatment of NRAS-mutated neurocutaneous melanocytosis with MEK162, a
      MEK-inhibitor.
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: BACKGROUND
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: >-
      Despite surgery, which is often not radical, few other treatment options exist.
    explanation: >-
      The state of treatment stated plainly by one of the groups trying to change it.
  - reference: PMID:24713450
    reference_title: >-
      Experimental treatment of NRAS-mutated neurocutaneous melanocytosis with MEK162, a
      MEK-inhibitor.
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: >-
      Further studies are needed to determine whether MEK inhibitors can effectively target
      NRAS-mutated symptomatic NCM, a rare but potentially fatal disease.
    explanation: >-
      The gap named by the authors of the one patient report that measured a pathway effect. No quote_role
      is recorded: this is a single-patient report's own call for future work, which is neither its
      finding nor somebody else's fact.
- discussion_id: ncm_mouse_allele_mismatch
  kind: HUMAN_MODEL_MISMATCH
  status: OPEN
  prompt: >-
    The mouse model of this disease carries NRAS G12D while every mutation-positive patient in the series that established the lesion - twelve of fifteen
    - carries a codon 61 substitution. Does the model's central nervous system melanoma arise
    the way the human one does?
  attaches_to:
  - pathophysiology#Post-zygotic NRAS Codon 61 Mutation
  - animal_models#Embryonic melanocyte NRAS(G12D) mouse
  rationale: >-
    The model is the main non-human evidence for this entry's lethal arm and it differs from the human
    disease in two ways that are not incidental. Its allele is at a different codon, and codon 61 is where the mutation sits in every
    mutation-positive patient of the series that established the lesion. And it is a transgenic rather than a mosaic, so the mutant cells
    are not surrounded by wild-type ones - which is the architecture the word mosaic in this disease's
    name refers to. The model's cutaneous lesions are reported as resembling blue naevi rather than
    congenital melanocytic naevi, which is consistent with the mismatch mattering rather than not. What
    the model does reproduce, early-onset central nervous system melanoma, is the result that made it
    worth building.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:23303902
    reference_title: >-
      Primary melanoma of the CNS in children is driven by congenital expression of oncogenic NRAS
      in melanocytes.
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: INDIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: >-
      NRAS is not a common driver oncogene of primary melanoma of the CNS in adults, but we report
      two cases of primary melanoma of the CNS in children, both of which carried oncogenic
      mutations in NRAS.
    explanation: >-
      The human side the model is held against: two children, in whom the paper does not say which
      codon. INDIRECT because the match between model and patients is what is at issue and this
      sentence does not close it.
notes: >-
  Every cited literature source is a case report, a case series, a retrospective or prospective
  cohort, a review of those, or a laboratory study. Each is cached as its abstract unless its cache
  file records a full text, and a statement here about what a source does not contain is a statement
  about that cached text. Orphanet's structured record for the disease, cited beside them, supplies
  most of the phenotype rows and most of their frequency bands. No randomised or controlled study of
  this disease was found, and wherever the entry uses a cited figure it names the denominator or
  says that the source does not, because the denominators differ in kind and the figures differ with
  them. Three recur among the cited studies. A screening series images every infant with the
  cutaneous lesion and so measures how common the neural lesion is in that group. A tertiary
  neuro-oncology referral series counts the children severe enough to be sent there. A pooled review
  of published cases counts the cases somebody chose to publish, which over-represents the severe;
  the Seizures, Hydrocephalus and Dandy-Walker bands come from it. Orphanet's frequency classes are
  a fourth kind, with no stated population and no denominator.

  The entry's four prevalence records among people with congenital melanocytic naevi span 4.8% to
  31.6%: prospective imaging of 38 Japanese infants gives 31.6%, a screened cohort of children born
  with more than one naevus gives 21%, a review gives about 12%, and a self-reported internet
  registry gives 4.8%. They do not measure the same thing - the 31.6% counts the lesion on brain
  MRI, symptomatic or not, the 21% an abnormal scan of any kind, the 12% a lifetime risk and the
  4.8% asymptomatic involvement only - so they are recorded side by side rather than averaged.

  Treatment evidence is case-level: the drug rows rest on reports of single patients or a few, on
  cells, or on both, and their explanations say which. Nothing in this entry should be read as
  evidence that a drug changes the course of the neural disease; whether inhibiting the pathway the
  NRAS mutation activates does so is the question the second discussion records.

  No ICD-O morphology is assigned. The lesion is an aggregation of naevus cells in the leptomeninges
  and brain parenchyma that can turn into melanoma, and the classification vocabulary's only
  melanocytic value is Melanoma, which would class the benign lesion as the malignant one.
review_notes: >-
  Written from cached sources; the classifications, the mappings and the placement of death in
  infancy also rest on the MONDO, NCIT, ICD-11 and HPO ontologies and on the schema's own
  vocabularies. The rows that transcribe Orphanet's phenotype table were generated from the cached
  record by a script; their notes, and everything else, were written by hand. Where a claim rests on
  one patient or on cells, the explanation says so, because a reader of a card sees the explanation
  and not the methods.

  Three things are deliberately absent. No reference range or laboratory threshold is recorded,
  because none of the cited sources gives one for this disease. No cell of origin is asserted beyond melanoblast, which is
  inferred from where the clone's descendants end up rather than observed, and the pathophysiology node
  says so. And the mutation-testing diagnosis row carries no diagnosis_term, because the nearest cached
  NCIT clinical-action term names immunohistochemistry and this row is a sequencing test; an
  approximately-right binding there would assert the wrong method.

Neurocutaneous Melanocytosis (MONDO:0009578) — Comprehensive Disease Research Report

Prepared: 11 November 2026 Target concept: Neurocutaneous melanocytosis / neurocutaneous melanosis (NCM), MONDO:0009578, OMIM:249400 Causal gene: NRAS (NRAS proto-oncogene, GTPase; hgnc:7989, 1p13.2) — present as a post-zygotic (somatic, mosaic) activating mutation, almost always in codon 61

Evidence-handling caveat for the curator. Every quotation below was retrieved from PubMed/PMC/OLS during this session, but several passed through a summarising fetch layer. Before any snippet enters a KB entry it must be re-cached with just fetch-reference <ID> and re-verified as an exact substring of the committed references_cache/ file. Treat every CURIE below as a lead, not a binding: the ones explicitly marked ✅ were resolved against OLS4, HGNC REST, the HPO annotation API, MGI/Alliance or OMIA in this session; the rest are flagged [verify].

1. Disease Information

Overview

Neurocutaneous melanocytosis is a rare, congenital, non-inherited mosaic neurocristopathy in which a single post-zygotic activating mutation — in the great majority of cases in codon 61 of NRAS — arises in a progenitor cell of the developing neural crest/neuroectoderm. The mutant clone populates both the skin (producing large, giant and/or multiple congenital melanocytic naevi, CMN) and the central nervous system (producing melanocytosis of the leptomeninges and/or brain parenchyma, and a range of non-melanocytic CNS malformations).

MONDO's own definition (OLS4, ✅ retrieved):

"Neurocutaneous melanocytosis (NCM) is a rare congenital neurological disorder characterized by abnormal aggregations of nevomelanocytes within the central nervous system (leptomeningeal melanocytosis) associated with large or giant congenital melanocytic nevi (CMN)."

The most authoritative recent clinical synthesis is Ruggieri et al. (PMID:33048248), whose abstract is quoted at length because it is the single best "what is this disease" statement in the literature:

"Neurocutaneous melanosis (NCM; MIM # 249400; ORPHA: 2481], first reported by the Bohemian pathologist Rokitansky in 1861, and now more precisely defined as neurocutaneous melanocytosis, is a rare, congenital syndrome characterised by the association of (1) congenital melanocytic nevi (CMN) of the skin with overlying hypertrichosis, presenting as (a) large (LCMN) or giant and/or multiple (MCMN) melanocytic lesions (or both; sometimes associated with smaller 'satellite' nevi) or (b) as proliferative melanocytic nodules; and (2) melanocytosis (with infiltration) of the brain parenchyma and/or leptomeninges." — Ruggieri M, Polizzi A, Catanzaro S, et al. Childs Nerv Syst. 2020. PMID:33048248

NCM is now best understood as one CNS phenotype within "congenital melanocytic naevus syndrome" (CMNS), a mosaic RASopathy — not as a free-standing disease of unrelated aetiology. Jaeger et al. explicitly file it among the mosaic RASopathies:

"...and mosaic conditions such as sebaceous nevus syndrome, neurocutaneous melanosis, McCune-Albright syndrome, phakomatosis pigmentokeratotica, epidermal nevus syndrome, and encephalocraniocutaneous lipomatosis." — Jaeger ZJ, et al. RASopathies. Part II: Cutaneous and extracutaneous manifestations. J Am Acad Dermatol. 2025. PMID:40532825

Synonyms and alternative names

From MONDO (✅): exact — NCM, neurocutaneous melanosis; related — NCMS, neurocutaneous melanosis syndrome. Also in use: neurocutaneous melanoblastosis, Rokitansky–van Bogaert syndrome (historical), melanophakomatosis, leptomeningeal melanocytosis with giant congenital naevus. Ruggieri et al. argue that "neurocutaneous melanocytosis" is the more precise term than "melanosis", because the lesion is a proliferation of melanocytes, not merely melanin deposition (PMID:33048248). The contemporary umbrella term for the whole mosaic disorder is congenital melanocytic naevus syndrome (CMNS).

Provenance of the information in this report

Aggregated disease-level resources and case-series literature only. There is no EHR-derived or individual-patient-data source for NCM at scale. The largest datasets are: - Great Ormond Street Hospital (GOSH) prospective CMN registry, 1988–present (PMID:18811688, 18671780, 25966033, 28078671) — the single most informative cohort; - the Nevus Outreach Internet-based registry (PMID:16310055); - a Japanese single-centre MRI series (PMID:37777590); - a 74-patient systematic review of neurosurgical management (PMID:35535823); - NCT06828822 (CINCLE — CongenItal Naevus Cohort for Longitudinal Evaluation), recruiting from 23 July 2025, which collects prospective neurodevelopmental and QoL data (ClinicalTrials.gov, ✅ API v2).

2. Etiology

2.1 Disease causal factors — a single mosaic oncogenic point mutation

The defining paper is Kinsler et al. 2013, which established causation:

"...oncogenic missense mutations in codon 61 of NRAS were found in affected neurological and cutaneous tissues of 12 out of 15 patients" ... "single postzygotic NRAS mutations are responsible for multiple CMN and associated neurological lesions in the majority of cases." — Kinsler VA, Thomas AC, Ishida M, et al. Multiple congenital melanocytic nevi and neurocutaneous melanosis are caused by postzygotic mutations in codon 61 of NRAS. J Invest Dermatol. 2013;133(9):2229–36. PMID:23392294, DOI:10.1038/jid.2013.70

Three features of that study are mechanistically decisive:

  1. The mutation is in codon 61 and is recurrent. "c.181C>A, p.Q61K" in 10 of 12 mutation-positive patients, "c.182A>G, p.Q61R" in 2.
  2. It is mosaic. The mutation was absent from blood and from clinically unaffected tissues — so it is not inherited and not germline.
  3. Skin and CNS lesions are clonally identical. All 11 melanocytic and non-melanocytic CNS lesions from 5 patients carried the same codon-61 mutation as that patient's cutaneous naevi. This is the evidence that NCM is one mosaic clone in two organs, not two diseases.

Charbel et al. independently showed the mutation is both near-universal in large/giant CMN and sufficient:

"Large-giant CMNs displayed NRAS mutations in 94.7% of cases (18/19)... We therefore performed exome sequencing on five specimens of large-giant nevi. The results showed that NRAS mutation was the sole recurrent somatic event found in such melanocytic proliferations... These findings strongly suggest that NRAS mutations are sufficient to drive melanocytic benign proliferations in utero." — Charbel C, et al. J Invest Dermatol. 2014;134(4):1067–74. PMID:24129063

Timing matters as much as the mutation. Charbel's small/medium CMN comparison group had a different genetic profile (70% NRAS, 30% BRAF), consistent with later, more restricted clonal origin. The large-CMN/NCM phenotype requires the mutation to arise early enough to be inherited by both cutaneous and CNS melanoblast precursors — i.e. at or before neural-crest specification/migration.

2.2 Risk factors

Genetic (all somatic / mosaic, none heritable):

Factor Evidence
NRAS codon 61 (Q61K ≫ Q61R) 12/15 patients (PMID:23392294); 94.7% of large-giant CMN (PMID:24129063); 77.3% of 66 patients, 75% of the NCM subset (PMID:25490715); 68% of a 2020 GOSH series (PMID:31111470)
BRAF V600E (minority alternative driver) Salgado et al. found BRAF V600E in 7.6% of 66 CMN patients and reported, as a novel finding, that BRAF V600E also occurs in large/giant CMN and in NCM (PMID:25490715). Polubothu et al.: NRAS 68%, BRAF 7% (mutually exclusive), double wild-type 25% (PMID:31111470). A 2026 case showed "sustained neurologic and cutaneous improvement following BRAF inhibition" in BRAF-V600E NCM (PMID:42384401)
BRAF fusion oncogenes A distinct CMN subset is initiated by BRAF fusion, with a characteristic firm, pruritic, desmoplastic phenotype (Roy SF, et al. Br J Dermatol. 2025. PMID:40036344)
Second-hit / progression events (risk of malignancy, not of NCM itself) Whole-genome copy-number gains and losses in progressive CNS disease (PMID:27933403); NRAS G12V acquired only in the malignant site, plus copy-neutral LOH (PMID:38254245); loss of H3K27me3 in 4/5 childhood nodular melanomas in CMN vs 0/20 proliferative nodules (PMID:27849631); GNAS copy gain + 10q loss in one malignantly transformed NCM (PMID:34994589)

Cutaneous phenotype as the dominant clinical risk factor for CNS involvement — this is the practical risk model:

  • Projected adult size (PAS) of the largest naevus, especially >40 cm: "MRI and/or clinical neurological abnormalities were found in 22 patients (18%) and were significantly associated with projected adult size of the CMN (particularly > 40 cm), and independently with male gender" (Kinsler VA, Chong WK, Aylett SE, Atherton DJ. Br J Dermatol. 2008;159(4):907–14. PMID:18671780).
  • Satellite/multiple naevus count. In the same study, "No complications were seen in the 16 patients with no satellite lesions at birth." More than 20 satellites is the usual threshold: "The presence of more than 20 accompanying nevi indicates an elevated risk of central nervous system (CNS) involvement" (Diagnostics. 2024;14(21):2345. PMID:39518313).
  • Multivariable Japanese data found significant associations of NCM with CMN distribution (p=0.002), size (p=0.01), satellite nevi count (p=0.002), rugosity (p=0.009) and nodules (p=0.006) (PMID:37777590).
  • Male sex is an independent risk factor for neurological complications (PMID:18671780); 76% of 74 published NCM patients were male (PMID:35535823).
  • Posterior axial / dorsal distribution is traditionally cited (80% of Agero's NCM cases had posterior-axis LCMN, PMID:16310055), but Kinsler's larger prospective series explicitly disputes it: "Importantly, there was no significant association between CMN distribution (including posterior axial location) and adverse outcomes" (PMID:18671780). This is a live disagreement in the literature and should be curated as such.

Environmental: essentially none established as causal. The mutation is a spontaneous post-zygotic event. The one epidemiological signal is weak and from a recall-prone design: "Other factors found to be significantly increased in CMN families compared with controls were maternal smoking and ill health during pregnancy. Maternal smoking was positively associated with PAS" (Kinsler VA, Birley J, Atherton DJ. Br J Dermatol. 2009;160(1):143–50. PMID:18811688). The authors themselves caution the study "may be subject to recall bias." There is no evidence for toxin, radiation, infectious or occupational causation. Age, sex and family history are not risk factors for acquiring the mutation (though 25% of CMN families reported a CMN in a second-degree relative, with a different gender ratio, "suggesting a different underlying mutation" — PMID:18811688).

2.3 Protective factors

No genetic or environmental protective factor is established. Two indirect observations:

  • Germline NRAS codon-61 mutation is presumed incompatible with life, which is why the disorder exists only in mosaic form. Germline NRAS variants at other residues cause Noonan syndrome 6 and autoimmune lymphoproliferative syndrome type 4 (Alliance of Genome Resources record for the NRAS ortholog, ✅) — i.e. a quite different, non-naevoid phenotype. This is an inference from the absence of germline Q61 cases, not a demonstrated lethality.
  • MC1R genotype modifies CMN colour (and therefore, arguably, photoprotection/surveillance), not CNS risk: "Final CMN colour in childhood is significantly associated with the individual's normal skin colour, and with MC1R genotype, and is therefore genetically determined" (PMID:31120141). Compound heterozygous MC1R risk variants have been reported alongside giant CMN (PMID:41624622).
  • Spontaneous lightening of CMN is common and is an argument against early surgery, not a protective factor against NCM (PMID:19244003).

2.4 Gene–environment interactions

None demonstrated. The maternal-smoking association with PAS (PMID:18811688) is the only candidate and is unreplicated and confounded. UV exposure is a general melanoma risk factor but has not been shown to modify melanoma risk within CMN, where melanoma characteristically arises in the dermis/subcutis or the CNS rather than at the dermo-epidermal junction — which argues against a UV mechanism for the CMN-associated tumours.

3.1 HPO annotations currently attached to OMIM:249400
